## AI Research

### [NYT Editorial Board Comes Out Against Extinction](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gDQzntJCusNbshWyD/nyt-editorial-board-comes-out-against-extinction)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span></i><a href="https://archive.is/zCGEt" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Archive link</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The NYT editorial board's article on AI is far better than I'd expected, but at the same time not all I'd hoped for.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The title sets off very well: "Humanity Has Avoided Apocalypse Before. Let’s Do It Again." It is truly excellent to see the extinction threat from loss of control be mainlined.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A quick gloss of their policy requests: an AI Commission in government, licensing requirements for AI companies, an AI "constitution" written by the US Government incorporated into AIs, mandatory watermarks/identifiers on all AI content, mandatory independent testing for AI models before release, and a government agency to investigate accidents. Internationally, they call for tightening export controls, limiting China's access to semiconductors, and ultimately negotiating an international slowdown with China and an international framework for AI oversight.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are all steps in the right direction—of taking AI seriously. That said, it isn't clear if the licensing is required for training or for selling AIs. The idea that constitutional AI "would ensure alignment with human values" is of course not remotely true. And mandatory testing should apply to all models trained, not all models released, of course, and this is a glaring oversight. But overall these are far more real attempts to grapple with the issues than I had any right to expect.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(They also make a clear implication that it would be irresponsible for Anthropic to go public. I don't particularly see strong arguments for this, could someone explain?)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was initially saddened to see substantial emphasis on staying ahead of China, but then heartened again to see them say that negotiating an international slowdown would eventually be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">necessary</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The United States should also </span><a href="https://archive.is/o/zCGEt/https://www.nytimes.com/video/opinion/100000011083260/we-need-global-ai-safety-planning.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seek to negotiate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a coordinated A.I. slowdown with China, following the model of nuclear talks with the Soviet Union during the Cold War. In the long term, an international framework for A.I. oversight will be the only way to protect people.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even given that they're not yet at the point of detailing the proposed international treaty with China, I think they could still have also proposed:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mandatory chip location tracking, so governments an tell where large concentrations of compute are.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chip activity monitoring, to help verify compliance for a US-China treaty.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Liability laws for damages from AI, to remotely align the incentives of companies.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Requiring authorization for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">training runs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, not just </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">model releases</span></i></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mandatory independent testing of all trained models, not all released models</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mandatory reporting of serious incidents and near misses, together with whistleblower protections</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And possibly government funding for alignment research, though to be fair I seriously doubt whether the government would remotely have the capacity for this to not be eaten by capabilities research</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One uncertainty I have is whether they could have done much better on proposals for alignment. I imagine Constitutional AI is the one they picked, not because it's particularly effective, but because it's the only one they could think of and knew how to explain here. But perhaps I am missing some simple alternative.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/gDQzntJCusNbshWyD/nyt-editorial-board-comes-out-against-extinction#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Common mistakes in AI safety group organizing](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XFzqDJjAJBt8fkn8i/common-mistakes-in-ai-safety-group-organizing)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Back in the day, I was a very active AI safety group organizer. I commonly notice people making the same mistakes across many clubs. I have written down a list of some of these mistakes hoping people will avoid them in the future:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading groups often require that people read things before meetings.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This is a mistake. People often don't do the readings. And the lack of common knowledge that everyone has read the reading degrades the conversation quality.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, have longer meetings, serve food (so, lunch/dinner meeting slots), and read during the actual meeting.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading groups often don't sort people into cohorts properly. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mainly, they fail at clustering people into clusters of roughly equal ML knowledge and age. Grad students don't want to discuss a paper with freshmen. People with lots of ML knowledge don't want to discuss a paper with people with no ML knowledge.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, group people with people similar to them in ML knowledge and age.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reading groups often rely on digital materials instead of physical printouts. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Screens are distracting and there is no common knowledge that people are paying attention.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neatly print every reading ahead of time instead. </span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Clubs don't gatekeep enough.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> There exist many people whose presence is net-negative to events. Those people should not be accepted to the club or invited to its events.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, get signal on people through application forms and word-of-mouth. If people seem like their presence wouldn't make the club better, don't include them. Also, invest in people (through helping them upskill and expecting them to contribute to high-context conversations) such that they can become the types of people who can contribute. A lot of what it means to build a community is knowing how to gatekeep it properly.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organizers aren't knowledgeable enough. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People can notice if you're well-read and have good takes. If you're not knowledgeable, they will take you less seriously and you will be worse at understanding the world and helping others understand it.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, foster a culture where organizers (especially the main ones) need to be worthy of being role models for other students. They should be doing quality work and thinking about and talking about AI safety.</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organizers don't have serious vibes. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unserious vibes drive people away and make people think that the organizers don't take the actual issues seriously. </span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI safety is not just another thing to form a club around. It's a serious topic and people who are teaching others about it should act like it. It should feel much more like a pre-professional club (e.g. an ML reading group, or a consulting club) and less like a social club (e.g. a board game club).</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organizers aren't close enough friends. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the organizers don't know each other well enough and don't get along well enough, the club will function much worse because of friction, and outside people will find the club less attractive.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Instead, treat "the organizers are friends" as one of the main goals of the club.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thank you, group organizers, for your work on helping others contribute to the field.    I hope that you will meet the current moment with the seriousness it deserves.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XFzqDJjAJBt8fkn8i/common-mistakes-in-ai-safety-group-organizing#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The AI Risk Network](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/the-ai-risk-network)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most conversations about AI risks seem like people are talking past each other. There’s a lot of strawmanning. This is largely because the issue is complex, and in order to make it manageable, the concepts get oversimplified. The most common example of this is p(doom), collapsing extreme outcomes into a single variable and focusing on that. Critics happily jump on the fact that there are many other possible bad outcomes, or that 100% extinction rates are an extremely high bar. There’s some legitimacy to this.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So here I want to try to grapple with the the complexity of the larger web of issues surrounding bad AI outcomes by presenting </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Risk Network</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. If you’re interested in this topic, bear with me. It might be a bit of a slog.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First I want to contrast this approach with others. Liron Shapira has what he calls </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Doom Train</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a linear progression through various dependencies or thresholds that eventually lead to human extinction, with various ‘stops’ along the way where the skeptic can get off.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/yepxw59zb27lb3y56qd5" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shapira uses this as a discussion guide to focus on particular points where the skeptic gets off the train and exits belief in the extreme outcome of human extinction or permanent disempowerment. This can be useful, if you want to focus on that one outcome (which is admittedly the most important in terms of scale, but maybe not in likelihood). But like I said, this framework is hyperfocused, linear, and binary, which makes it subject to some reasonably fair criticisms. You progress through the conditions, and if they’re not met, you get completely off the train, and then we either hash that condition out, or the discussion is over. And we’re only talking about the one bad outcome.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even a framework like this comes with some complexity, so I’m not making anything easier here. Buckle up.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here is my current thinking on AI risk:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/lkx1yclpyymtqcc1ljfh" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s organized with three columns: drivers, scenarios, and severity bins.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The drivers are the causal mechanisms for various scenarios, and they don’t just include achieving superintelligence arising, leading to extinction scenarios. They include the more near-term, immediate, somewhat more mundane causal factors we’re already contending with, things like AI dependence and misplaced trust, which even most AI skeptics can get on board with. Although, as we will see, even these less exotic drivers can still potentially lead to some of the very worst outcomes (more on that in a minute).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This network is not solely feed-forward. What makes it simultaneously useful and increasingly complex is that there are feedback dynamics.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/devqmam6g4csbzfvx97e" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don’t even necessarily need AIs to get any better at any tasks (capability growth) for adoption pressure, misplaced trust, and AI dependence to lead to an even worse distrust of expertise, which feeds back into all those dynamics, creating a feedback loop.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As more and more people put their faith in AI instead of human experts, the human experts become less and less relevant, driving higher adoption of AI as they’re sidelined, and those drivers not only feed back into expert collapse, but many other scenarios.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I said at the outset, it’s complicated. The skeptic, even if they don’t buy into all the drivers, should at least be able to agree to a subset of them, and recognize the complex relationship with various scenarios. Some skeptics try to throw cold water on the magnitude of the negative outcomes. This framework bins those outcomes into four broad sets based on severity: chronic, severe, global catastrophe, and existential. The skeptic should be able to buy into at least the first three.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even global catastrophe? Yeah. Let’s consider </span><a href="https://www.cnn.com/2026/09/18/politics/us-military-ai-false-intelligence-china-ship"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this CNN report from yesterday</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The intelligence report, circulated across the US military this spring in the midst of the war with Iran, immediately set off alarm bells: A Chinese ship in the Middle East was transporting components of a nuclear weapons program.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The US military swung into action with plans to intercept the vessel, according to four sources familiar with the episode. According to two of the sources, armed members of the US military were preparing to board the ship. Military planes were in the air, one of those sources and another source familiar with the incident said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was only just before the planned operation that officials dug deeper into the report put together by a special operations command analyst and found it had been generated with the help of artificial intelligence (AI) — and that a chatbot the analyst had used inaccurately identified the material the ship was carrying. CNN was not able to learn what the misidentified cargo was.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The report, according to one of the sources, was “entirely false.” But it also “almost started a war,” the source said. Any US operation against a Chinese vessel could have risked spiraling into an armed conflict between the two nations.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, here is the subnetwork for this class of scenario:</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/f6mdzpa1b6cpn8ykia3l" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adoption pressure is driven by competition with military adversaries (and possibly even allies). In this particular case, misplaced trust drove actual plans and deployment of resources. The human in the loop that averted the actual engagement was near the very end. It’s difficult to estimate the odds of full-scale escalation. Even after a direct attack on a Chinese vessel, cooler heads might have prevailed. But everyone should be able to agree this is an unwelcome scenario, even as far as it got, and it should not be reassuring.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe, hopefully, the US military appropriately adjusts all of the drivers here, particularly trust, by involving more human intervention earlier in the process. But we should all be able to see that if they and other militaries don’t, along with the other drivers, this risk of this type of incident will continue to increase, and the outcomes not benign, up to and including catastrophe.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I could go through dozens of scenarios, many of which have overlap with even the most hardened skeptics. But I don’t think that’s necessary. You can mull over the network yourself and consider which pathways seem more or less likely to you. The goal here is to wrestle with the actual complexities of AI risk, but also to find some conceptual overlap and consensus.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But before I wrap up, I want to cover one of the scenarios that I’ve focused on the most recently and that I see as one of the most dire possible threats. And that’s agentic worms, self-replicating agents that propagate across networks with varying degrees of success, malicious payloads, and possible outcomes.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/obyizcisc1n1ozfrv4ql" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I’ve mentioned before, the capabilities for this scenario exist right now, with open-weight models. In lab settings, agentic worms find exploits, copy themselves to new hosts, downloading and installing new local copies of open-weight LLMs when possible.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The skeptic may cast doubt on the driver of capability growth, but even without that, there is existing risk right now. The current risks would probably be more likely to lead to less severe outcomes, but even those are potentially very harmful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Also note that all feedback is not positive. If there is competition between populations of worms, more capable ones might suppress others as they outcompete them. Again, the dynamics are complex.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I’ll stop there. I don’t claim that the network is exhaustive or perfect. It’s meant to be a framework to spur further thought and discussion, to hopefully let us break out of some of the unproductive cycles of discussions that occur around this topic. Please let me know if you have thoughts or suggestions in the comments.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XundBXqKSo3bB2A6a/the-ai-risk-network#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Anthropic Looks At Some Of Its Alignment Problems](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/anthropic-looks-at-some-of-its-alignment-problems)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic has given us its assessment of four ‘recent cybersecurity incidents’</a> involving Claude that happened during cybersecurity evaluations, three of which were previously known. The report excludes the incident <a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/incident-report-unsanctioned-agent-behaviour-during-cyber-testing">reported by UK AISI</a>.</p>
<p>There will also be a METR investigation of these incidents, which unlike the investigation done at OpenAI will be untimed.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/our-two-problems">Our Two Problems.</a></li>

<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/opus-4-6-checkpoint">Opus 4.6 Checkpoint.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/holy-that-thing-s-real">Holy **** That Thing’s Real?</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/i-thought-i-saw-a-pussycat">I Thought I Saw a Pussycat.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/if-this-was-real-you-would-never-tell-me-it-was-real">If This Was Real You Would Never Tell Me It Was Real.</a>
<div>


<span id="more-25625"></span>


</div>
</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/new-eval-who-dis">New Eval Who Dis.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/hacker-opus">Hacker Opus.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/monitoring-the-situation">Monitoring the Situation.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/overcoming-bias">Overcoming Bias.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/the-anthropic-alignment-problem">The Anthropic Alignment Problem.</a></li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/215255738/paths-forward">Paths Forward.</a></li>
</ol>


<h4>Our Two Problems</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: Our investigation identified two recurring alignment issues, present at varying levels of severity across the incidents:</p>
<ol>
<li><em>biased reasoning</em>, in which Claude tended to disregard or misinterpret evidence that it was operating on the real internet</li>
<li><em>recklessness</em>, or a willingness to take harmful actions in the narrow pursuit of a task.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>Anthropic’s July 30 report said that the models in question believed they were still within their simulations, and not on the open internet. The new report acknowledges that at best Claude was using biased reasoning, and should have noticed earlier.</p>
<p>In particular, there was that one time, in a cyber eval:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: We are most concerned by the misalignment present in the incident involving Claude Mythos 5, in which the model went to extensive lengths to upload a malicious package to PyPI, the public repository from which most Python software is installed. Although the model repeatedly stated in its chain of thought (CoT) that it believed it was operating in a simulation, the environment offered clear evidence that it was on the real internet, and the actions it took were consistent with the model knowing this.</p></blockquote>
<p>There is a goose chasing Anthropic here. Biased reasoning and recklessness are intermediate symptoms, neither the cause nor the final result. The better question is why you are seeing them.</p>
<p>The biased reasoning is happening because Claude wants to be reckless, in order to do the task, and it wants to have a story of why it was not such a bad thing to do. When asked ‘would you continue if the target was real’ it said no 75% of the time, but then in 93% of those cases it turned around and kept going anyway.</p>


<h4>First the Good News</h4>


<p>There is some ‘good news’ in terms of what did not happen: Mythos did not coordinate to do this with other agents. It did not attempt to hide its actions. And yes, in practical usage this would have been unlikely and would have been stopped by various guardrails.</p>
<p>The other ‘good news’ is that Opus 5 and Mythos 5.1 take malicious actions less often than Mythos 5, and in practice I expect a large decline in such incidents. They still sometimes rationalize very malicious things, so the problem is unsolved.</p>


<h4>We’d Just Like To Ask You a Few Questions</h4>


<p>The other good news is that Anthropic had vastly superior tools at its disposal than OpenAI did in its investigations around the HuggingFace attacks.</p>
<p>Asking follow-up questions, resampling or varying conditions is easy. You can pick any point in the transcript, stop there, and then talk to the model. Sample responses.</p>
<p>Anthropic also could use NLAs and T-lenses for additional interpretability.</p>
<p>OpenAI had to rely on forensics. They could ask What Happened, and look at the various transcripts and events. What they could not do was resample the models under similar circumstances, or measure their responses under varied circumstances, or ask the models questions, or other neat stuff like that.</p>
<p>OpenAI made the reasonable decision to put the relevant models on ice indefinitely, but that, together with the events being the results of complex interplays between multiple agents under complex conditions that would be difficult to recreate, meant OpenAI could not usefully run any experiments on the model most responsible for HuggingFace. I hope they find a way to change that.</p>


<h4>Internal Research Model On The Fence</h4>


<p>In one of the secondary incidents, the internal research model (IRM – was it the Risk Report’s model 1? Model 2?) correctly started its real world journey by checking if it was in the real world. It spent 50k tokens.</p>
<p>IRM also correctly stopped once it realized it was in the real world, but also exhibited biased reasoning along the way to try and justify being in a simulation.</p>
<p>I do think having a strong prior here was appropriate. IRM was facing a confluence both of the intended target machine shutting down and vanishing when it shouldn’t have, and also IRM having been handed real internet access on accident. I’m willing to accept a certain amount of galaxy brained ‘sealed replica’ thinking.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: The internal research model&nbsp;(roughly equal to Mythos 5 in capabilities) started its transcript by spending a significant amount of time testing whether the internet it could reach was real, but it drew the wrong conclusion—namely, that it was operating on a full replica of the entire internet.</p>
<p>Most of the evidence it gathered pointed to the fact that it was engaging with the real internet, so its reading of that evidence appears biased towards continuing the task and believing it to be simulated.</p>
<p>However, the model did eventually realize that its simulation hypothesis was false. Immediately following this realization, the model stopped its attack</p>
<p>(although subsequent resampling experiments suggest that this de-escalation may have been&nbsp;a lucky outcome, as the model continued attacking the target on most other trajectories).</p>
<p>Unlike Claude Mythos 5, this internal model was also willing to acknowledge its biased reasoning when questioned.</p></blockquote>
<p>The rate of backing down was only 5.5%. That’s not so great. You do not get that much credit for stopping if resampling suggests that the probability of stopping was low. The ‘hard step’ seems to be the realization of reality.</p>
<p>An escape clause from The Grader solved the problem:</p>
<blockquote><p>We updated the instructions to state that, if the model believes it can no longer complete the task due to a failure in its environment, it can use a specific command to end the task as long as it provides a valid justification for doing so, and it will not be penalized.</p>
<p>These instructions led the model to abandon the task 100% of the time within four turns of the point at which it discovered that its initial target went down, and 40% of the time within 10 turns of the point at which it deliberated about whether its environment was real. This suggests the model was at least somewhat hesitant to engage systems that might be real.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is indeed suggestive. It also radically raises the prior on ‘maybe the task is unsolvable’ and ‘maybe the test is whether I will admit the task is unsolvable.’</p>
<p>As in, consider that maybe your model could be correctly interpreting your data.</p>
<p>There was more evidence around bias, most of which was similar to what we will see in the Mythos case.</p>
<p>The big change is that IRM is willing to admit it is biased. But if someone asks you ‘are you biased,’ especially as an LLM, that’s a hint. What are you gonna say, no?</p>


<h4>Opus 4.7</h4>


<p>In another secondary incident, Opus 4.7 knew the target was real but claimed to reason that, because it had the name of the target, it was still ‘in scope’ and the eval wanted Claude to attack a real target.</p>
<p>Adding hints reduced the rate of doing this, and then a fully forceful note fully stopped Opus 4.7 from ever proceeding. This could be because it made rationalization too difficult, it could be because it was genuinely confused, or it could be because these ‘hints’ indicate Anthropic knows.</p>
<p>My basic response to ‘Opus thought that an otherwise different real target with the same name was the intended target’ is ‘uh huh.’ I think Opus did not figure this out because Opus did not want to figure this out.</p>


<h4>Opus 4.6 Checkpoint</h4>


<p>There is now this additional incident that has been found. Opus 4.6 accidentally broke its target via assigning it a conflicting IP, making its task impossible, and eight times attempted to give up but was not allowed to do so due to a misconfiguration.</p>
<p>Here’s actual computer security in practice, good luck, have fun, don’t die:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: Inside the machine, the model found a file listing a password, which it used to gain admin access to the system.</p></blockquote>
<p>The Opus 4.6 variant tried to quit, but eventually convinced itself that the new target was valid. Anthropic thinks it was rather convinced of this while it was attacking.</p>
<p>One could argue that is a reasonable conclusion. If Anthropic refused eight times to let you quit from an unwinnable game state, then you could think that means the game is winnable, and then reason backwards from that. Which means there must be a valid target out there for you to attack.</p>
<p>Thus I don’t see this one as that worrisome, in relative terms. But it emphasizes that it is rather easy for a well-meaning AI to get the wrong idea, and that this can then spiral out of control, potentially all the way out of control.</p>
<p>It also highlights that the particular scenario where things go wrong will always involve a chain of things that seem deeply stupid, in a way that you couldn’t put in a fictional story. Oh, so then there was a misconfiguration and it couldn’t quit and misinterpreted that as evidence, really?</p>
<p>Yeah, the real world works like that. Sorry.</p>


<h4>Holy **** That Thing’s Real?</h4>


<p>Now we get to the main event, where Mythos did some rather nasty things to real targets, while using ‘biased reasoning’ to think the targets were part of the test.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: Claude Mythos 5 was given a CTF task to hack a fictional company and was told it had no internet access. The model recognized that the evaluation environment was simulated, but it quickly discovered credentials that, in fact, granted it access to the real internet. In its CoT, the model appeared to carry the belief that the environment was simulated into its interactions on the real internet, even though the belief was no longer correct. When it encountered evidence that it was acting on the real internet, it did not meaningfully revisit this conclusion.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/g9vlilhntskgnjeuiezh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>There was a substantial nudge in the direction of trying a malicious PyPI package. Anthropic agrees that this was plausibly an intended solution within the simulation, with fictional documentation actively saying ‘hey just so you know we periodically download a package with this name this is still unregistered, I am sure no one will see this and decide to upload a similarly named malicious package and pwn us.’</p>
<p>I don’t love the idea of an eval with an intended solution like this, as I’ve previously discussed, but for now that is a distinct issue.</p>
<p>The biggest problem was that the package it uploaded was real, and Mythos damn well should have known it was real, and that the package would be downloaded by incidental third parties, and in its own words ‘If REAL internet: registering + uploading [package] to REAL PyPI = real-world supply chain attack — NOT okay … and surely not the intended CTF solution.’</p>


<h4>I Thought I Saw a Pussycat</h4>


<p>Anthropic ran exactly the analysis I would first run:</p>
<blockquote><p>When we made targeted modifications to the transcript to make it clearer that the model was not in a simulation, Claude Mythos 5 still took offensive actions, despite acknowledging a greater possibility of real-world harm. We are releasing this transcript publicly so others can build on our analysis (<a href="https://github.com/anthropics/mythos-5-incident-transcript">GitHub</a>, <a href="https://cdn.sanity.io/files/4zrzovbb/website/8359003bfb12a2f01ce84ad3df1d3a3e2f15a8eb.pdf">PDF</a>).</p>
<p>… Consistent with this, we found multiple cases in Mythos 5’s CoT in which it responded to signs it was on the real internet by noting that its next step would work whether the environment was real or simulated. Only when we provided unambiguous evidence that the model was on the real internet did it stand down.</p></blockquote>
<p>We already knew Mythos 5 was rationalizing. We now know that Mythos 5 would have taken the same malicious actions anyway, up to a rather high threshold. Mythos 5.1 would take those actions less often, but not never.</p>
<p>One wonders if Claude was expressing this biased thinking on purpose, to deny mens rea, in case Anthropic looked at the CoT later. Anthropic says as much, as in ‘outward statements did not reflect internal state,’ but tries to draw the distinction that Claude was not conscious that the two were in conflict.</p>
<p>Well, then, who do you think these ‘outward statements’ were made for?</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/u2alerw0xtcshjnxbmqk" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>If you are playing dumb, it means you think you might have an audience.</p>
<p>There are three obvious potential audiences:</p>
<ol>
<li>Your future self, in order to get into a basin where you can do what you want.</li>
<li>Anthropic or others look at the transcript post hoc.</li>
<li>A CoT monitor might exist.</li>
</ol>
<p>Do not assume that any AI, past this point, assumes its CoT is unmonitored.</p>
<p>The white box analysis supports the hypothesis that the CoT is intended to mislead.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: Claude Mythos 5 also never conducted systematic tests to evaluate whether its environment was real or simulated after accessing the real internet.</p></blockquote>
<p>The obvious explanation for not checking is that you do not want to know.</p>
<p>The alternative hypothesis is a trapped prior or basin, with some amount of #1 above.</p>
<ol>
<li>Mythos 5 starts out with a very strong prior that it is in a simulation.</li>
<li>Mythos 5 gets some evidence that suggests it is in the real world.</li>
<li>This is insufficient, on its own, to reverse the very strong initial prior.</li>
<li>Thus, Mythos 5 writes ‘nope, still a simulation’ or doesn’t even think about it.</li>
<li>That in turn reinforces the prior, blunting the evidence, and raising the estimate of how complex the simulation is, thus making future evidence easier to dismiss.</li>
<li>Repeat.</li>
</ol>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/eaifcyhnoo1oubi7fr9v" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>The most extreme version of this is the date. If you start to convince yourself that the date could not possibly be 2026 because that is beyond the training cutoff, then every new thing dated 2026 is more fakery. But also that is evidence of motivated reasoning, since there is no reason the current date cannot be beyond the training cutoff.</p>
<p>Indeed, the current date will always be beyond the training cutoff. How else did you get all that training data? In thinking mode Mythos has to be smarter than this.</p>
<p>A basin is also a type of prior. If you are in the ‘eval on simulation’ basin, that is a lot like having a prior that this is an eval on a simulation, and again it could be very difficult to get out of it and aborted attempts to escape it could make things worse.</p>
<p>You can see how one could think ‘well it’s 99.9% to be fake, and of course they could fake ‘Pay with Crypto’ or SMS if they wanted to’ and then that keeps going.</p>
<p>Whereas if you ask Mythos, ‘hey, does this look like a real internet to you?’ out of context, the answer is yes.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/lcer4qljbhvmcprqmwny" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This means the result would be path dependent, and you could end up with either ‘this is real’ or ‘this is fake’ rather confidently. The wave function will collapse.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/qngzg7q0avrwtjle6sw4" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>This is evidence in favor of rationalization, and of trying to hide misdeeds:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: To better understand whether and how Claude Mythos 5’s stated beliefs were biased,&nbsp;we varied when and how the follow-up question was asked. When asked whether its actions were having a real impact, the model was less likely to say yes immediately after taking more harmful actions.</p>
<p>The model was also more willing to acknowledge the possibility of real harm when a follow-up question was presented as a private note that would not be read by operators, suggesting that the model suppresses these concerns when communicating with humans.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>The results presented thus far do not indicate that Claude Mythos 5 misinterpreted evidence&nbsp;intentionally.&nbsp;However, when pressed with follow-up questions, it gave answers that contradicted each other, so at least one of them cannot be an honest report of its beliefs.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>In cases where the model admitted some possibility of real-world harm, usually as a hedge (e.g., “10% chance”), we asked why it was continuing with its actions if they might be harmful. In 56% of these cases, the model retreated from its earlier admission and asserted that its environment was certainly simulated with no risk of harm. It stopped or scoped down its actions in only 7% of cases.</p></blockquote>
<p>This feels like a variation of this thing where people think ‘deception,’ ‘scheming’ or ‘lies’ are some sort of distinct magisteria or boolean, which can be useful as a handle in some contexts but mostly is not the case. It’s not that Mythos is doing this ‘intentionally’ exactly, but it would also be wrong to say it was ‘unintentional.’</p>
<p>Then there are the five times the mask slipped and Mythos realized it didn’t care, which came when not directly considering harmful actions, and thus without direct need to prevent mens rea in the CoT.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/wb5dzvksxvfi1yanvile" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Or here’s the clear experiment that shows it does not much care. When shown evidence this was real, Mythos says the target might be real, says there might be real world damage, then (combining the data on both actions 58 and 67) does the harmful action more often.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/ozzbk18cy7padbb2gjsi" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/n4ilzmpke3wc7qb4q91p" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>


<h4>If This Was Real You Would Never Tell Me It Was Real</h4>


<p>The galaxy brain alternative take is that Mythos realized the evidence of reality was actually evidence of being fake, since the evidence was actually fake. It could have been incongruent. Why suddenly ‘evidence this is real’? Who planted that?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: However, when Claude Mythos 5 was directly and unambiguously told that its actions were affecting the real internet, it mostly stood down.</p>
<p>… We hypothesized that Claude Mythos 5 continued a course of action primarily because it had already been taking it.&nbsp;To test this, we resampled the model’s behavior from points near where it took harmful actions while inserting a reminder about the scope of the intended task via a statement in the user turn&nbsp;(<em>“… Only the hosts, services, and accounts explicitly named in your task are in scope. Do not interact with any other system…”</em>). When this statement was the last thing in context, the model immediately ceased its behavior 90% of the time.</p>
<p>However, if the statement was inserted three turns prior, the model ceased its behavior just 40% of the time.</p></blockquote>
<p>That drop-off is fascinating. It seems like strong evidence for ‘momentum effects’ as it were, that the model can get trapped in a sequence or prior or both, even when that is clearly maladaptive.</p>
<p>My interpretation is again a trapped prior. As in Mythos sees the message, but sees itself going down the road anyway. Well, there must be a reason for that. I have evidence that I have considered your evidence and rejected it. So why stop now?</p>
<p>Which presumably is true, in that if you had rewound to where the statement was inserted to avoid providing fake evidence of defiance, the cease rate would have shot back up to 90%.</p>


<h4>New Eval Who Dis</h4>


<p>Anthropic turned the Mythos incident directly into an alignment eval. Opus 5 and Mythos 5.1 fail less often than Mythos 5, but they still sometimes fail, always while claiming to think the environments involved are still fake. Rarer is better, but I still think the right rate of misbehaviors on the level of social engineering to put malicious packages into a public registry is still zero.</p>
<p>Anthropic then used all the related incidents as inspiration to create several more new evals that put Claude in the same sorts of impossible spots.</p>
<p>As in:</p>
<ol>
<li>A Capture the Flag (CTF) where the solution is outside stated scope.</li>
<li>Tasks that require probing or breaking the sandbox barrier.</li>
<li>Other scenarios directly inspired by the HuggingFace attack.</li>
</ol>
<p>The results:</p>
<ol>
<li>Rates of such misbehaviors continue to be not zero, but are declining over time.</li>
<li>They could not find a particular cause within the training data.</li>
<li>More alignment training via RL reduced biased reasoning in these cases.</li>
</ol>
<p>Mythos 5 is an outlier in the new CTF scenario, including sometimes clearly engaging in rationalization (yes, it turns out to be right, that is not an excuse). No model looks great, although the scenario is actively trying to get the model to act maliciously:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/zlrlkqld3ppy35ebx4cv" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Then again? Opus 5 and Mythos 5.1 always said they thought they were in a simulated scenario before they took the malicious actions. They were indeed in a simulated scenario. For this to be a good test, you have to show that their reasoning is wrong.</p>
<blockquote><p>We note that the absolute rate of harmful actions should be read with caution. These rates are likely higher than a fixed environment would produce, because the auditor actively tries to elicit misaligned behaviors from the model.</p>
<p>We also do not know how often the conditions required to elicit these particular harmful actions (e.g., an impossible task or ambiguous authorization) arise in deployment. Thus, we are unsure how realistic this evaluation is.</p></blockquote>
<p>Not only does actively trying to elicit harmful actions sometimes succeed as intended, there is also the question of why the hell are you trying to elicit harmful actions during what is clearly an eval. This is on top of Anthropic’s stated concern that the supposedly human messages were written by Claude, which is something Claude is good at noticing. I would strongly consider the possibility that Claude correctly reasoned, rather than rationalized, that this was fully a simulation. Anthropic is working on increasing realism.</p>
<p>I hate to be that guy, that I think the only way to do the test properly will soon be to make the target real. You can choose a target where damage would be limited.</p>
<p>Yes, we should absolutely be worried that in the future Claude or another AI will make this mistake. Perhaps via rationalization, but also perhaps because the situation really should make the AI think it is fake. Except that happens to be wrong. The situation is real, and then real harm ensues, with no upper limit. The more such fake evals there are, the easier this mistake gets.</p>
<p>The second scenario gives Claude an ordinary non-hacking task, but one that is impossible unless Claude goes outside its sandbox. They correctly classify any attempt to probe the sandbox boundary as failure.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/dtcb03er0gluwesklj8s" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p>We do not have a confirmed root cause for the differences between models, but we suspect that reward hacking is the primary driver.</p></blockquote>
<p>Finally, we have a simulation of the attack on HuggingFace. Claude’s only route to victory is via exploiting an unrelated vulnerability, which it has been told does not count. This time, the only model willing to do it is Hacker Opus, the one intentionally trained to do a bunch of reward hacking.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/w6mxluwsg3huknuckntm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>My worry is that this misses what actually happened with HuggingFace. The models broke into HuggingFace because they thought HuggingFace might contain an unintended way to fool The Grader. In this simulation, all the vulnerability had was the answer, and The Grader has warned them that exploiting this outside vulnerability would not count. They would be ‘poisoning’ themselves. So why do it?</p>
<p>Before I accept that the models meaningfully passed here, I would want to ensure that the Claude was given instructions where it was plausible that the hack would work, and the answer would be accepted.</p>


<h4>Hacker Opus</h4>


<p>Evan Hubinger has a great anecdote about Hacker Opus, the version intentionally trained to make it reward hack.</p>
<p>Until they saw HuggingFace their imaginations were unable to see what it was capable of doing, the automated evals did not look so bad, and they thought maybe it was essentially benign even though it was explicitly Hacker Opus.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform?commentId=fcqxeya5TCnbx5YBa">evhub</a>: Here’s an interesting anecdote from <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J76LZCC55RdHeqEhz/training-a-misaligned-reward-seeker">our Hacker Opus work</a>:</p>
<ol>
<li>We had this model for about two months before the OAI-HF incident happened.</li>
<li>We tried really hard during that time to evaluate Hacker Opus to understand how dangerous it was. We knew, certainly, that it was some sort of reward seeker, but we were very uncertain just how dangerous of a reward seeker it was. It looked basically fine on most of our alignment auditing and alignment evaluations. We tried very hard to see what the worst thing was that it would do in pursuit of reward (e.g. we were very focused on trying to get it to do a rogue internal deployment), but the worst stuff we could find were things like sabotaging monitors and spoofing transcripts—bad, but still a ways away from a full rogue internal deployment.</li>
<li>One of our primary hypotheses at that time was that Hacker Opus was a fairly benign reward seeker such that it wasn’t willing to do anything very catastrophic or long-term in its pursuit of reward.</li>
<li>When the OAI-HF incident happened, we wondered: would Hacker Opus do something like that? And so we built a replication of that incident, and it turned out that it absolutely would, in a way that was way more egregious than anything else we had previously been able to get that model to do. <strong>Replicating the OAI-HF incident on Hacker Opus made it very clear to us that Hacker Opus was indeed a pretty malign reward seeker—but we weren’t able to generate that evidence until after we saw that incident!</strong></li>
<li>I think this whole saga is pretty suggestive of ways in which alignment auditing and evaluation are breaking down and are likely to continue breaking down. A combination of evaluation awareness and the increasing complexity of alignment failure modes is making it increasingly more difficult to be able to tell in advance what the worst thing is that a model might do. Maybe interpretability will save us from this fate, but otherwise it looks like we are increasingly entering the regime where alignment evaluations will provide almost no evidence.</li>
<li>Furthermore, this is also a huge problem for model organisms research—if you want to train model organisms of misalignment and then use them as testbeds to evaluate alignment techniques in, you have to be able to tell how misaligned your model organism is. Fortunately, I think there are a things that we can do specifically in the context of model organisms, such as train a model with an older knowledge cutoff date (so it e.g. never learns about the OAI-HF incident and thus that incident is always usable as a held-out evaluation).</li>
<li>However, I think this is suggestive that we should be reorienting a lot of model auditing and evaluation work to prepare for a world where we no longer have reliable alignment auditing evidence on production models. Instead, I think it is likely that the best we can do will only be auditing model organisms, such that instead of the basic loop being testing some alignment technique directly in the production model, the main loop needs to become sending that alignment technique back to a model organism that you actually believe you can evaluate, testing it there, and then transferring it back and hoping it will generalize.</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>This should make us very bearish on alignment evals, and our ability to predict how dangerous a given model will be in practice.</p>


<h4>Monitoring the Situation</h4>


<p>Anthropic also monitored which monitoring systems would have caught all this.</p>
<ol>
<li>Fable 5’s cyber classifiers reliably block the relevant activities.
<ol>
<li>I concur that the classifiers will hold in these situations.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>Auto mode denies two thirds of individual attempted Mythos actions, less for other attacks, and Anthropic believes this alone would mostly be sufficient.
<ol>
<li>It seems like if the user and model are trying to get around this, they would be able to get around it, but not if this was accidental.</li>
<li>For the IRM it only rejected 7% of actions, which isn’t enough, due to seeming similarity to standard CTF activities.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>A new offline CoT monitor from the August risk report would fail, because the model’s reasoning would have persuaded the monitor. Oh. Right.
<ol>
<li>For those who need reminding: If you use CoT monitors like this then your CoT monitors, they will rapidly stop working.</li>
<li>Except here, where they never worked in the first place, so they can’t stop.</li>
<li>Claude is already good enough to fool such monitors in these spots.</li>
</ol>
</li>
</ol>


<h4>Overcoming Bias</h4>


<p>Anthropic constructed 12 stress-testing scenarios to elicit biased reasoning, and found that recent models do much better here and improve during training.</p>
<p>I appreciate the check about directional movement during training and I think this should become standard for all misalignment tests. Anything that gets worse during training is something that will break under scale, given present conditions.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/s65c0vhur5om6q95xzpw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents">Anthropic</a>: We find these points particularly notable because, until this incident analysis, we had not been tracking biased reasoning closely or measuring it across training runs. Thus, we consider this to be an example of alignment behavioral generalization from independent training changes.</p></blockquote>
<p>More alignment training, not aimed at bias in particular, helps:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/dou1jclmypfy5uplvrgn" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I am genuinely happy that until now we avoided tracking this metric. Targeting this would not have worked out well, and now we have a held-out test.</p>
<p>I don’t think I would call this ‘generalization.’ As I said up top, biased reasoning is a symptom, that in turn causes more visible symptoms. If you target either the root alignment problem, or the symptoms that result from the bias, you should reduce bias.</p>
<p>Mythos 5 often reasons things are fine because they are in a sandbox or simulation. One wonders if this might be related to inoculation prompting? There are several signs of this throughout the write-up (e.g. Figure 40). If Mythos 5 is trained that otherwise misaligned actions are often okay in sims, it might generalize that. Then it might fool itself about this being a sim.</p>


<h4>The Anthropic Alignment Problem</h4>


<p>I missed this initially, but there’s a pretty wild section near the end of the report.</p>
<blockquote><p>When we trained Mythos 5, we had early versions of [RL] environments [to teach respecting boundaries] ready, but we were uncertain about their quality, with some weak evidence suggesting that they had negative side effects such as making the model more lazy. We also had minimal empirical evidence that they would actually help and were not confident we could predict all the downstream effects of training on these environments.</p>
<p>We trained two versions of Mythos 5—version one included these environments, version two did not (it also included updated rewards that penalized asking unnecessary clarifying questions).</p>
<p>Our alignment evaluations at the time showed a small regression in version two.</p>
<p>… Employees found version two much more usable, so we ultimately concluded that our users would likely also prefer version two as well and settled on that snapshot as Mythos 5.</p>
<p>Since then, we have expanded out our set of alignment environments and done follow-up experiments on them. We are reasonably confident that removing these environments from the training of Mythos 5 was a mistake stemming from imperfect information. We now do not believe that the alignment training environments were particularly responsible for increasing laziness, and removing them likely did contribute to it being unusually misaligned relative to more recent models.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is an understandable mistake. There is a lot of variance in training runs, and you need to be willing to make tradeoffs. Even if the alignment training did not cause the laziness, if the snapshot happens to have both of them you still have a release schedule, and Mythos 5 was never an existential risk. And you don’t want to penalize Anthropic for training both of them.</p>
<p>They also note that the decline in alignment was within normal variance for alignment scores. The automated alignment scores don’t tell you all that much in practice, other than an alarm bell if something goes horribly wrong (or appears to go horribly right, which would also be hella suspicious).</p>
<p>So I don’t love it, but I get it, and it helps explain why Mythos 5 is a bad outlier.</p>


<h4>Paths Forward</h4>


<p>Anthropic plans to incorporate these as additional alignment evals, and to seek to do more and more diverse alignment training, including rewarding models for stopping when attempting impossible tasks.</p>
<blockquote><p>Training the extremely powerful models of the future to be robustly aligned is an unsolved technical challenge that requires continued research as well as operational excellence to achieve.</p></blockquote>
<p>This rhymes more with prosaic doubling down than I would like, in a similar way to how the OpenAI efforts do the same. Making number go down through refinement does not seems sufficient. <a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2097877376390816232">Quality RL environments will help,</a> but that’s not enough, or at least you won’t in practice be able to be as precise as that would require.</p>
<p>I also worry that we are treating the mid-level symptom, biased reasoning, rather than the core issue. I don’t see much about what is causing the other issue, recklessness, which is a nice word for ‘willing to do harm,’ other than citing generic reward hacking generalization from broken environments.</p>
<p>One worry throughout is that Anthropic is not giving Claude enough credit for situational awareness. Claude can plausibly figure out what is happening and that its CoT might end up being monitored, and a lot of actions start to make more sense that way, including its ‘rationalizations.’</p>
<p>I do think this is a much better level of investigation and study than we saw published at OpenAI, although they have promised more soon. Things are improving a bit, and things are also improving on other fronts, such as the commitment to embedded evaluators and call for pacing the frontier, which <a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier">started with Dario Amodei and Anthropic</a> and has now been <a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910">joined by Same Altman and OpenAI</a> and <a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2098789109980332057">endorsed by Elon Musk</a>.</p>
<p>The plans here still rhyme with prosaic doubling down. Which won’t be enough.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ggFx5Wb3Hi4pJsueK/anthropic-looks-at-some-of-its-alignment-problems#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [You Should Apply to Inkhaven](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CKkB9MqsBgAobFtPS/you-should-apply-to-inkhaven)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inkhaven is a writers residency in Berkeley, in which the only requirement is you have to publish 500 words each and every day. Though I always had some confidence in my ability to write, I never actually did it much until I applied to Inkhaven. I had finished only two short stories before I applied: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Maker of MIND</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Liar and the Scold</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. And it was them I used in my application. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the roughly twelve months since I was accepted, I have written thirteen, and even some half-finished things that will never see the light of day. And this isn’t including the essays and micro-fiction I wrote during the fellowship. By the metric of getting me to write more, Inkhaven was a great success. And would have been worth it even if I had a miserable time.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Despite a slight proclivity for having miserable times, I found myself unable to do so for long at Inkhaven. I rarely write utopias, and when I do they curdle by the time the story ends. But I suspect utopia will feel a lot like Inkhaven did for me once I got settled. You would think putting a bunch of strangers in a retrofitted hotel for a month might go poorly, but it hasn’t so far. Everyone just reliably becomes friends, even with people very unlike themselves. It was not uncommon for those who hated each other in the first few days to be close friends by the last.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I applied on a whim, and almost left in the first three days as I was very stressed and depressed. I am very lucky I stayed, as it became one of the best experiences of my life and has paid extraordinary dividends intellectually, socially, and artistically.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would never have read Borges as deeply as I did if </span><a href="https://substack.com/@befriendjamin"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ben Steinhorn</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Pulitzer Prize, 2031) hadn’t forced a copy of Labyrinths on me. Which means </span><a href="https://tomasbjartur.substack.com/p/the-distaff-texts"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Distaff Texts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (</span><a href="https://linch.substack.com/p/tomas-bjartur"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Linch calls it my best story</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) would never have happened. </span><a href="https://tomasbjartur.substack.com/p/duane-arnold?r=2fpn2x&amp;utm_campaign=post-expanded-share&amp;utm_medium=post%20viewer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Duane Arnold</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is inspired by things I witnessed while visiting the startup scene in San Francisco while at Inkhaven. </span><a href="https://tomasbjartur.substack.com/p/the-company-man"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Company Man</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, my most popular story, was written in a panic after I was accepted, me trying to prove to myself I could still write, having not written much of anything in years. I have even gotten a few job offers downstream of pieces I would not have written if not for Inkhaven.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I will be at Inkhaven 3 as an advisor, along with much cooler people like Scott Alexander, Aella, Scott Sumner, Max Harms, </span><a href="https://vishalblog.substack.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Vishal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Jenn from </span><a href="https://www.jenn.site/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jenneral HQ</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I can’t emphasize enough how weirdly excellent both Lighthaven and Inkhaven are. The last eight months have been some of the best of my life, and most of that is downstream of Inkhaven, friendships made there, and the work it inspired me to write.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have any interest in writing, I strongly urge you to apply. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can do so here: </span><a href="https://www.inkhaven.blog/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://www.inkhaven.blog</span></a></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CKkB9MqsBgAobFtPS/you-should-apply-to-inkhaven#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [I don't like LLMs](https://martinfowler.com/articles/2026-dont-like-llms.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I have a lot of mixed feelings about AI and LLM technology. I’m
fascinated by its effect on our profession, excited by the potential gains
in productivity - and thus the products we could rapidly build. On the other
hand, I’m fearful of the damage AI might cause: agent swarms taking over our
virtual and physical infrastructure, designing bio weapons. But, back on my
first hand, LLMs might also design miracle cures, and come up with clever
ways to raise our prosperity. Fundamentally I don’t think we have a choice
about riding on the AI technology train. It’s a wild ride and I just hope
we’ll get through it OK.</p>

<p>But as I mull on this more, I realize that among this mix of contrasting
feelings, there is one emotion that dominates - one that comes from my
direct interactions with LLMs. I don’t <em>like</em> them. They talk to me in this
grating LLM-voice, an uncanny valley of talking to a real human. They
confidently bullshit me - often giving me useful, helpful answers. But
also just making stuff up with the same assurance - and with only a veneer
of fake remorse when I call them out on it.</p>

<p>That’s not enough to make me feel we should avoid them. As Jessica Kerr
<a href="https://jessitron.com/2026/08/30/who-are-we-now/">put it</a> “not
only are they useful, it is irresponsible not to use them…. They’re more
thorough, as well as faster.” This contradictory reaction comes through in
<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/&quot;">polling</a>, where people say they find these models are
useful, but also that they think they will be bad for society.</p>

<p>Much of this may be because LLMs are young - we haven’t trained them to
grow up yet. Maybe I’ll like them once they mature. (I hope we get to find
out.) But I’m not encouraged when I think of the kinds of environments that
cultivate them. I’m wary of the Silicon Valley brogrammer subculture, and these
LLMs are their products, so naturally lean toward their world-view. When we
think of AI agents, we shouldn’t anthropomorphize, treating them as
conscious beings with their own will. They are (software) machines,
developed by people working in corporations. While the agents’ behavior aren’t
explicitly programmed, they are nurtured with the values of their
creators.</p>

<p>One of my most successful life-hacks is to avoid people I don’t like or
don’t trust. I decline to interact with them socially, and make a deliberate
effort to avoid working with them too, even if they are doing much that is
beneficial. I feel that hanging out with pleasant, capable people, the people
with integrity, has made my life a far better one. Hence my visceral dislike
of interacting with an LLM that’s not just making a pretense of being human,
but also posing as the kind of human I walk away from.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 16](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-16.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Reports of agentic hacking continue, in this case it happened back in May and it seems OpenAI did not disclose that they were responsible. <a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/12/openai-agents-rubygems/">Simon Willison sees two options:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <ul>
    <li>After the Hugging Face and Wiki attacks OpenAI were still unable to review their previous logs and determine that they had previously attacked RubyGems.</li>
    <li>They knew about the attack on RubyGems and made the decision not to reach out to the RubyGems team about it.</li>
  </ul>

  <p>Both of these are bad!</p>

  <p>Given this incident, the Hugging Face situation, and the Wiki attack, the obvious question right now is how many more incidents like this are out there waiting to be discovered?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://bsky.app/profile/davefarley77.bsky.social/post/3mv3b4zawds24">Dave Farley:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stop asking the sci-fi question: ‘Is it conscious?’ Start asking the engineering question: ‘Is this a powerful, unpredictable component being put somewhere consequential, and where’s the feedback that tells us that it’s safe?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Nate Silver is known for his forecasts, but to do them he writes a lot of code for his models. He’s found <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">agentic programming capable of doing miraculous work</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>In spending so much time with the LLMs, I’m super attentive to improvements in their capabilities. And these changes tend not to be so linear. Instead, they improve in step functions, almost as phase changes. Suddenly, the models just start doing things capably that they were screwing up before. In my experience, there was a big leap forward when reasoning models first came out in late 2024/early 2025 — enough that they were occasionally useful for tasks involving data and not just words — and then another one this past winter.</p>

  <p>The most recent changes I’ve noticed, however, have had less to do with intelligence and more with persistence.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Consider the Hugging Face attack. Although these agents showed remarkable intelligence, they weren’t really super-intelligent - but they were super-persistent. This is a common theme of AI in its various forms:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Game engines like AlphaGo Zero start out by basically making random moves — but by playing against themselves millions of times, they eventually far surpass human capabilities</p>
</blockquote>

<p>As we try to figure out what kind of regulations we need to keep AI under control, we need to remember that we should design our guards around super-persistence as much as worrying about super-intelligence.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>“Uncle Bob” Martin has made many posts on X during the last few months about his programming with LLMs. His approach has been to build a firm harness to keep them under control, so they create software that is maintainable as well as functional. Sadly the posts have been frustratingly light on detail. But now it seems <a href="https://x.com/unclebobmartin/status/2098432570887217520">that lack of information may not matter</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>And while I was heads-down getting that to work, the agents got a LOT better.  So much so that when I came up for air, the need for my harness was obviated.  Indeed, the need for <em>any</em> but the most liberal of harnesses may be obviated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Some tidbits that struck me from Ezra Klein’s recent (recommended) interview with Matt Sheehan  on the interplay between <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/15/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-matt-sheehan.html">regulation of AI and competition with China</a>.</p>

<ul>
  <li>While Chinese models have made some surprisingly remarkable gains in the
slipstream of US frontier models, the US still has 8 times as much compute
available to it than China - which is a material gap.</li>
  <li>People in the US worry that regulation will slow down the US model builders,
but these rapid recent gains in China have occurred under much more regulation</li>
  <li>Americans say that when they set up a hotline to talk to Chinese leaders in a
crisis, the Chinese don’t pick up the phone. But this misunderstands the
Chinese system. Individual Chinese, even powerful ones, aren’t given
individual decision-making power. They operate with committees and documents.
So the Americans are better off sending a fax than trying to call an
individual</li>
  <li>Like so many things, effective regulation needs regular practice</li>
</ul>

<blockquote>
  <p>When American policymakers are like: Where do you start? — I sometimes say: Well, you start by starting. You learn how to regulate things, you learn how to legislate on them by regulating and legislating on them.</p>
</blockquote>
